Personalized learning assessment for secondary education using a hybrid model of circular intuitionistic fuzzy and Aczel–Alsina bonferroni aggregation operator
Authors: Raiha Imran, Kifayat Ullah, Hanen Karamti, Zeeshan Ali, Izatmand Haleemzai
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
Personalized learning (PL) achievement in secondary schools is often questioned because it does not always lead to improved student performance, as it has consistently lacked strong data-driven models that can effectively address the uncertainty in student data. Although teachers tailor the lessons to meet the requirements of individual students, it is always challenging to measure if these efforts improve learning. This is because student preferences, strengths, and weaknesses generate a significant amount of unclear and complex data. To manage these complexities, our study introduced an innovative and novel decision-making approach to determining the effectiveness of personalized learning, utilizing the proposed Circular Intuitionistic Fuzzy Aczel–Alsina Bonferroni means (CIFAABM) aggregation operator (AO). This approach enables us to combine multiple data sources, which reveals the impact of PL on individuals, captures the interrelation between various factors, and provides a more comprehensive and precise way to assess student progress. In this paper, we integrate the Bonferroni Mean (BM) AO by using the Aczel–Alsina (A-A) operational rules within the circular context, which provides a more realistic way to evaluate these assessments. The proposed approach demonstrates its practical application in optimizing personalized learning paths and enhancing student engagement by accurately assessing these complex criteria. Its effectiveness is validated through comparative analysis with existing methods. The results validate the use of our model, which impacts the accuracy of PL evaluations for educators in a scalable and empirical decision support framework.Supplementary InformationThe online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1038/s41598-025-08970-8.

Introduction

In recent years, the field of education has undergone significant changes, marked by innovative adjustments to pedagogical strategies, curriculum design, and teaching methods. Understanding from the cognitive sciences and psychology, which have emphasized the importance of attending to individual learning needs and differences, has substantially influenced this evolution. As a result, many researchers now consider PL1 to be of critical importance. Conventional teaching approaches often prioritize uniformity, which can overlook the unique needs and individual specifications of each student. The ability of learners with varied learning styles, speeds, and interests was not adequately accommodated by this one-size-fits-all approach. Therefore, personalized learning seeks to address this by tailoring learning experiences to meet the distinct needs of each student, thereby enhancing learning outcomes. Technology’s rapid advancement has become game-changing, making the PL model more effective and reliable. By utilizing real-time data and analytics, modern technologies enable teachers to tailor their lesson plans and courses of study to meet the individual needs of students more effectively.

The American PL Initiative2 emphasizes the importance of technology in enabling a large-scale PL. This initiative aims to highlight the significant role of modern technologies in truly personalizing education and transforming educational institutions. These technologies are crucial because they facilitate the collection and analysis of student performance data, which is used to develop individualized teaching strategies tailored to the needs of each student. Compared to typical uniform approaches, PL represents a significant shift from established educational systems, favoring more customized and flexible approaches. Utilizing innovative technologies enables the customization and scalability of PL experiences, which are essential to successfully implementing this strategy. Therefore, to fully realize the potential of PL and eventually provide every student with a more effective and engaging educational experience, these technologies must continue to be developed and integrated. PL emerged as an innovative approach to revolutionize education by tailoring instruction to meet the unique needs of each student. To ensure the success of this approach, it is crucial to develop a comprehensive assessment framework that evaluates factors such as student engagement, individualized learning paths, learning outcomes and performances, and technological integration. To assess PL, a mathematical assessment approach yields a practical recommendation for continuous improvement, identifying key elements that effectively promote PL. Furthermore, effective assessment techniques ensure that PL strategies align with academic goals and enhance teaching strategies to elevate learning outcomes and student enthusiasm. So, the PL is evaluated by a team of experts, who consider the multiple criteria and can be regarded as an MCGDM problem.

In various facets of our daily lives, decision-making is essential. It is the process through which experts carefully analyze all available options and select the most optimal one. Initially, experts used concrete values to analyze the data. However, as time passed, multiple criteria were introduced, and the process became more intricate, leading to inaccurate evaluations using real numbers. Then, the shortcomings of the classical set theory3 were highlighted due to the human tendency towards ambiguity and uncertainty. In response to this challenge, Zadeh4 introduced the concept of fuzzy sets (FS) to address imprecision and vagueness in data. This innovative approach enables experts to convey their degree of fulfilment (membership) concerning the quality of an alternative within the closed unit interval [0, 1]. The FS theory does a good job of explaining vagueness, but cannot adequately characterize the level of disagreement (non-membership) n. Considering these limitations, Atanassov5 presented the intuitionistic fuzzy set (IFS), which is more stable and effective than FS because it can handle both m and n degrees. Several researchers have tried to handle intuitionistic fuzzy data by applying different AOs, including Alcantud6, and Wang and Feng7. Later, Yager8 extended FS and IFS into the Pythagorean fuzzy set (PFS) concept. In PFS, the squared sum of the degrees of m and n lies between 0 and 1. Moreover, various researchers have studied PFS theory and its practical applications9–11, etc.. However, some real-world problems have not yet been solved by IFS or PFS. Then, Yager12 introduced q-rung orthopair fuzzy sets (q-ROFSs) to handle such cases. With these sets, experts have more freedom to express their opinions because, in q-ROFS, the sum of the qth power of m and n lies between 0 and 1. In particular, it is the generalization of IFS and PFS; when q = 1, it corresponds to IFS, and when q = 2, it corresponds to PFS. Several researchers, including13–15, among others, have focused their efforts on exploring research challenges within the q-ROFS framework due to its various advantages and the ability to handle uncertainty with greater precision. Quantifying the degrees of IFS by using a single numerical value is difficult. So, to solve this problem, Atanassov expanded IFS to include an interval-valued intuitionistic fuzzy set (IVIFS)16, where the sum of the upper bounds lies between 0 and 1. However, as research progressed, the related challenges gradually emerged more clearly. Jia et al.17 presented the interval-valued q-rung orthopair fuzzy sets (IVq-ROFS) for these realistic scenarios. By utilizing a parameter q > 1, these sets modify the constraint on upper bounds such that the sum of the qth power of upper bounds lies between 0 and 1. As a result, compared to IVIFS, IVq-ROFS provides experts with more freedom to voice their opinions. Afterwards, another challenge emerged for the experts: expressing their views while considering the circular environment. So, Atanassov18 introduced the concept of circular intuitionistic fuzzy set (CIFS), which deals with the membership degree m and non-membership degree n, along with the radius r, providing experts with a new direction to express their opinions more comprehensively. Table 1 below presents an analysis of CIFS with FS, IFS, PFS, and q-ROFS. By considering the ability to deal with more complex problems, various researchers have utilized their efforts to explore the challenges within the CIFS, as noted by Pratama et al.19 suggested the operations of CIFS, Ashraf et al.20 proposed the time series forecasting methodology, Kamber et al.21 defined the CODAS method within the circular environment for the selection of the green logistic Ambiguity

Handling

In decision-making, the advancement of FS has been greatly aided by T-norm (∆) and T-conorm (∇). Many AOs come from different ∆ and ∇. For instance, Sarfraz22 proposed the Maclaurin Symmetric mean AO based on the Frank ∆ and ∇, Qiyas et al.23 defined the confidence level by fuzzy AO, Seikh and Mandal24 defined the IFS-based Dombi AO, Seikh et al.25 presented the evaluation of e-learning using Dombi AO, Ullah et al.26 proposed a decision-making method by utilizing the frank prioritized AO and Seikh and Mandal27 defined the frank AO with unknown attribute weight. Moreover, several researchers in fuzzy mathematics studied Hamacher ∆ and ∇, which resulted in the creation of Hamacher AOs and their use in multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) scenarios. Javeed et al.28 proposed the decision-making approach based on Hamacher AO, Zeb et al.29 utilized the Hamacher AO to optimize the electric power system selection, as did Ali et al.30 proposed the assessment of goldmine using Hamacher AO. Then, researchers have also investigated Frank31 ∆ and ∇, which are generalizations of many ∆ and ∇, such as Hamacher, algebraic, and Einstein ∆. But as compared to prior ∆ and ∇, Aczél and Alsina32 presented a flexible pair of ∆ methods ∇ to facilitate the MCGDM problem. Senapati33 proposed the A-A AO-based outranking method, Siab et al.34 proposed the A-A AO under linguistic terms and then further investigated A-A AOs for MCGDM applications35. However, no literature currently links CIFS with A-A ∆ and ∇ under BM AO. By considering this, in this paper, we aim to develop the circular intuitionistic fuzzy Bonferroni mean (CIFBM) AO by utilizing A-A ∆ and ∇ creating an effective tool for CIFS that would improve the ability to handle more imprecise MCGDM issues and provide decision-makers with more flexibility. Moreover, the A-A operator is handy in MCDM problems when decision-makers have to combine criteria that are more or less imprecise or involve a certain level of uncertainty. This operator enables decision-makers to control the sensitivity of the aggregation process by changing its parameters, thus presenting them with an aggregation tool that can be flexibly adapted to different decision environments.

In CIFS, the A-A AO has been used to aggregate CIFVs, which applies to augmenting both the magnitude and the direction of such values. This is especially important when criteria are linked in a circular or non-linear manner. Xu et al.36 also demonstrated the efficiency of the A-A AO in CIFS-based models, which enhanced the reliability of decision outcomes compared to those based on IFS. Furthermore, Senapati et al.35 employed A-A AO to address decision-making problems that involve uncertainty and fuzziness. Then, for further generalization, the A-A AO has been utilized within the q-ROFS environment, which various researchers have subsequently utilized to solve MCDM problems. The main advantage of the A-A operator is that it allows for more detailed aggregations due to its flexibility, which is particularly helpful in situations where the criteria are interconnected and complex. The Bonferroni Mean (BM) operator is an AO originally built upon Bonferroni’s work, Carlo Emilio Bonferroni37, an Italian Statistician best famous for the Bonferroni inequalities and probability theory. Yager38 further encouraged the BM operator to consider the interactions between pairs of inputs instead of managing each independently; this feature is vital in MCDM since criteria can be interrelated. This operator has also been generalized and incorporated into other fuzzy set contexts to address even more complex decision-making issues that involve uncertainty, vagueness, and interdependence. Then, an intuitionistic fuzzy BM (IFBM) operator39 is utilized in MCDM contexts. It incorporates the BM into the IF framework, allowing for greater flexibility and enabling adjustments according to the chosen level of interactivity of the criteria used in the BM operator. This research has, therefore, brought a different perspective on the advancements of fuzzy BM operators, which enhance the availability of flexible and robust aggregation tools for accommodating interdependent criteria in complex decision-making environments.

The BM operator, responsible for modelling interactions between criteria, and the A-A AO, responsible for handling different degrees of uncertainty, are two of the most common operators in the fuzzy logic field. Nevertheless, the contribution of this paper lies in proposing a combination of BM and A-A AO within a single framework, specifically designed for personalized learning environments. While prior works employ these operators independently or embed them within even simpler decision-making frameworks, we combine the BM operator’s awareness of criterion interdependencies with the A-A AO’s flexibility in addressing uncertainty. Such a combination comes into play to provide a deeper and more resilient decision-making that can adapt to the individual, multiple-criteria environment of personalization. While such a framework has not been extensively discussed in the literature, the present research contributes to filling the gap by presenting novel theoretical and practical contributions that strengthen the appropriateness and performance of personalized learning paradigms. Moreover, there is increased flexibility and effectiveness in addressing ambiguity and dependency, improving on current approaches, and offering a new way of approaching learning design under uncertainty.

Literature review

As a revolutionary approach to education, PL adapts teaching strategies and materials according to each learner’s requirements and preferences. This literature review looks at the theoretical basis and development of PL, highlighting important findings and essential elements from earlier research and comprehending the motivations behind the practice. This section will give a thorough analysis of the current study.

Overview of personalized learning

Adapting the learning process is an innovative educational approach according to the demands of each student’s needs, preferences, and abilities, and is known as PL40. In the modern world, we can develop an environment that is more productive and captivating with a student-centered approach, which is crucial for fostering the development of vital abilities that are required for success. PL empowers students to take charge of their education by encouraging self-paced development and freedom41. Through individual strategies, learners can broaden their knowledge to align with their goals. By integrating the technology into PL environments, many opportunities have popped up that cater to the needs of each student. Digital tools improve information accessibility, promote teamwork, and simplify data management. Digital learning environments help teachers meet specific learning objectives by allowing for the customization of assessments, learning pathways, and adaptive testing strategies.

PL represents a major technological innovation with the potential to transform education42. To enhance their understanding of PL, educators can delve into its various facets, including PL policies, small-group instruction, project-based learning (PBL), student mentorship, the role of teachers, comprehensive student development frameworks, and success habits. By integrating these elements, educators can fully leverage the advantages of PL environments. Combining personalized learning with diverse instructional strategies and addressing social and emotional aspects creates a powerful approach to meeting each learner’s unique needs. Teachers can investigate the various facets of PL to improve their understanding and implementation. Here, include the following facets below;Personalized Learning Policies: These create rules and structures that facilitate the customization of educational experiences. These regulations ensure that individualized learning is consistently implemented under educational standards and goals.Small-Group Instruction: Organizing learning in smaller groups enables educators to deliver more concentrated and individualized attention. This strategy addresses particular student requirements and promotes a more collaborative learning environment.Project-Based Learning (PBL) involves including students in projects that demand critical thinking, problem-solving, and teamwork. PBL enables students to apply their information in practical, real-world situations, making learning more meaningful and relevant.Student Mentorship: In student mentorship, everyone is assigned a mentor who provides guidance, support, and encouragement. Mentorship assists students in navigating their educational journeys, setting objectives, and staying motivated.Role of teachers: Teachers facilitate individualized learning by creating and providing tailored learning experiences. They evaluate student progress, provide feedback, and tailor training to meet various learning requirements.

Role of teachers: Teachers facilitate individualized learning by creating and providing tailored learning experiences. They evaluate student progress, provide feedback, and tailor training to meet various learning requirements.

Educators can optimize the benefits and efficacy of the PL strategy by adding these features, combining several instructional methodologies, and focusing on the emotional and social components, resulting in a practical framework for PL. This comprehensive approach guarantees that all learners must fulfill their specific needs and be satisfied, which results in a more inclusive and supportive educational experience. The purpose of PL is to support the entire growth of students rather than just academic outcomes. Instructors may create a more exciting and successful learning environment by considering different learning styles and preferences. PL empowers students to take control of their education, facilitates self-paced exploration, and fosters intrinsic drive, leading to a more enjoyable experience. Figure 1 defines the essential components of PL implementation, including learner characteristics, environment, and outcomes.Fig. 1Components of PL implementation.

Components of PL implementation.

The challenges linked to PL models, including the issues with learning style and vagueness in student learning progress, are assessed by the CIFAABM since its capability to cope with uncertainty and imprecision. CIFAABM permits the different criteria to be aggregated by considering their varying degree of membership, which reflects the characteristics of complex education environments. This model can integrate numerous inputs, ranging from students’ preferences to their performance, to develop a more accurate and holistic evaluation that will help teachers improve students’ performance suitably for their needs. The CIFAABM AO provides a sophisticated mathematical approach to address the complexities of PL in secondary education. Unlike traditional fuzzy aggregation methods that may oversimplify interdependencies among criteria or fail to accommodate non-linear relationships, the CIFAABM AO provides a more comprehensive framework for capturing these complexities, ensuring a more accurate and nuanced evaluation of PL models. It effectively manages diverse and ambiguous data by aggregating multiple criteria like learning styles, engagement, and responsiveness, offering a flexible and nuanced analysis that traditional methods may lack. Its ability to handle circular dependencies and non-linear relationships among these criteria enables educators to better understand and respond to the unique needs of students. By leveraging the CIFAABM AO, educators can enhance decision-making processes and improve educational outcomes by effectively managing uncertainty and diversity in student learning paths. Combining these advanced mathematical models with practical PL strategies ensures that each learner’s characteristics, environment, and outcomes are aligned for a more comprehensive and effective educational experience.

In the case of PL environments, especially in secondary education, educators experience practical problems like accommodating the students’ learning styles, working with students who display different levels of enthusiasm to learn, and handling situations where there is uncertainty in evaluating students’ performance. The proposed CIFAABM AO directly offsets these challenges by properly steering the FS data’s uncertainty and vagueness when and as required to address the purpose of evaluating PL models encompassing multiple interacting criteria. Compared to conventional approaches, CIFAABM AO provides a better understanding of such diverse, rather vague data as learning trajectories and students’ active involvement. By offering versatile and strong mathematical support, the CIFAABM AO can help educators make more sound choices and optimize the educational approaches that can be better fitted to individual learners’ and students’ progress, improving the efficiency of PL models.

Gap in literature and motivations

Despite continuous improvements, the literature still has several gaps. To assess the efficacy of the PL model, we require a comprehensive framework that utilizes data-driven techniques, such as MCGDM. Furthermore, the purpose of the learning environment and its specific features still require further research for their implementation. To address these gaps, we aim to provide more accurate techniques for assessing and enhancing PL experiences, ensuring that they are equitable and effective for all learners.

The key research gaps are presented below;There is a lack of a comprehensive model for assessing the efficiency of PL that employs data-driven methodologies, such as MCGDM. Therefore, it necessitates the development of robust assessment frameworks that enable teachers to effectively evaluate various facets of PL and ensure that initiatives are grounded in trustworthy data and aligned with educational objectives.The specific criteria necessary for an efficient implementation of PL models must be investigated. By employing an MCGDM approach, teachers can identify the most suitable criteria for planning and implementing professional learning (PL) strategies.The significance of student interaction and learning environment in PL approaches is not appropriately analyzed or evaluated. Understanding how student engagement affects learning outcomes emphasizes the importance of creating innovative and motivating learning environments to engage students in their schooling.

The motivations of this research are presented below;Existing models do not establish the relationship between CIFS and A-A T-norms and T-conorms in the context of BM operators. The CIFAABM operator fills this void by incorporating improved fuzzy environments and operations, making it more versatile and reliable within decision-making contexts.The work aims to provide a new approach to assessing PL models in educational settings, where various factors and outcomes, including students’ activity, affordances, achievement, learning pathways, and trajectories, must be considered in a complex manner. The CIFAABM operator is useful in supporting this assessment in some way, as a reliable evaluation of such performance is crucial.With the help of the developed CIFAABM operator, the paper goes further to deliver improved flexibility and accuracy in dealing with the imprecise and uncertain information required by decision-makers in real-world applications in areas such as education, health care, and other fields that require the development of decision models that can cope with imprecision.The proposed operator aims to enhance MCGDM’s capabilities by incorporating interdependence relations between multiple criteria more appropriately. This helps to make a more accurate assessment and better decision, as the criteria are interdependent and can be determined with a high degree of difficulty.

Existing models do not establish the relationship between CIFS and A-A T-norms and T-conorms in the context of BM operators. The CIFAABM operator fills this void by incorporating improved fuzzy environments and operations, making it more versatile and reliable within decision-making contexts.

The work aims to provide a new approach to assessing PL models in educational settings, where various factors and outcomes, including students’ activity, affordances, achievement, learning pathways, and trajectories, must be considered in a complex manner. The CIFAABM operator is useful in supporting this assessment in some way, as a reliable evaluation of such performance is crucial.

The work aims to provide a new approach to assessing PL models in educational settings, where various factors and outcomes, including students’ activity, affordances, achievement, learning pathways, and trajectories, must be considered in a complex manner. The CIFAABM operator is useful in supporting this assessment in some way, as a reliable evaluation of such performance is crucial.

With the help of the developed CIFAABM operator, the paper goes further to deliver improved flexibility and accuracy in dealing with the imprecise and uncertain information required by decision-makers in real-world applications in areas such as education, health care, and other fields that require the development of decision models that can cope with imprecision.

With the help of the developed CIFAABM operator, the paper goes further to deliver improved flexibility and accuracy in dealing with the imprecise and uncertain information required by decision-makers in real-world applications in areas such as education, health care, and other fields that require the development of decision models that can cope with imprecision.

The proposed operator aims to enhance MCGDM’s capabilities by incorporating interdependence relations between multiple criteria more appropriately. This helps to make a more accurate assessment and better decision, as the criteria are interdependent and can be determined with a high degree of difficulty.

The proposed operator aims to enhance MCGDM’s capabilities by incorporating interdependence relations between multiple criteria more appropriately. This helps to make a more accurate assessment and better decision, as the criteria are interdependent and can be determined with a high degree of difficulty.

Contributions

This study aims to contribute to the field by designing and implementing a multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM)- based framework for evaluating personalized learning models. By emphasizing data-driven decision-making, this study will provide insight into the most critical criteria for effective implementation, the value of student participation, and the role of the learning environment. Ultimately, the findings will provide teachers with practical ideas for enhancing personalised learning practices, leading to a more successful and inclusive educational experience. Thus, the main contribution of the paper is as follows;The development of a comprehensive framework under CIFAABM AO using MCGDM to evaluate the PL model and identify the most crucial factor for effective implementation of PL.To discuss the properties of the defined framework under CIFAABM AO.To determine the role of student involvement in PL and provide perspectives on how student engagement affects learning outcomes and the overall efficacy of PL strategies.Enhance PL experiences by emphasizing data-driven decisions that address individual student needs and preferences.To analyze the impact of learning environments on PL models, emphasizing the importance of tailored learning environments in increasing student engagement and learning results.To assist teachers by providing a strategy for improving PL practices to be successful, equitable, and inclusive to all students.

The development of a comprehensive framework under CIFAABM AO using MCGDM to evaluate the PL model and identify the most crucial factor for effective implementation of PL.

To discuss the properties of the defined framework under CIFAABM AO.

To determine the role of student involvement in PL and provide perspectives on how student engagement affects learning outcomes and the overall efficacy of PL strategies.

Enhance PL experiences by emphasizing data-driven decisions that address individual student needs and preferences.

Organization of study

The paper is organized as follows. Section II discusses CIFS and their operational laws, utilizing the A-A ∇ and ∇ and BM AO. Section III discusses the Aczel Alsina Bonferroni mean (AABM) for Circular Intuitionistic Fuzzy Numbers (CIFN) and their properties and special cases in detail. Section IV illustrates the Multi-Criteria Group Decision Making (MCGDM) technique based on the derived operator and applies it to selecting the best and simplest procedure for evaluating personalized learning (PL). Section V presents examples to compare the proposed operators with existing ones, expanding on the attractiveness of the new operators. Finally, Section VI offers concluding remarks.

Preliminaries

This section reviews key concepts of C-IFS, BM, and A-A, along with their operational rules, to assist readers in understanding the basics. Circular intuitionistic fuzzy Aczel–Alsina bonferroni mean operator (CIFAABM)

In this section, we are currently developing a very efficient and reliable AO, i.e., CIFAABM operator, by implementing the fundamental concepts of BM AO and A-A operational rules.

Theorem 2: (Idempotency)

Consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{\mathsf{i}}=\left({\text{m}}_{\mathsf{i}},{\text{n}}_{\mathsf{i}},{r}_{\mathsf{i}}\right) ;\left(\mathsf{i}=\text{1,2},\dots ,\mathsf{n}\right)$$\end{document} be a collection of CIFNs. Then,

16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\text{CIFAABM}}_{\Delta }}^{{\ell},\mathcal{m}}\left( {C}_{1},{C}_{2},\dots , {C}_{\mathsf{n} }\right)=C$$\end{document}

Similarly, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\text{CIFAABM}}_{\nabla }}^{{\ell},\mathcal{m}}\left( {C}_{1},{C}_{2},\dots , {C}_{\mathsf{n}}\right)$$\end{document}.

Theorem 3: (Monotonicity)

Consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{\mathsf{i}}=\left({\text{m}}_{\mathsf{i}},{\text{n}}_{\mathsf{i}},{r}_{\mathsf{i}}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{i}{\prime} = \left( {m_{i}{\prime} ,n_{i}{\prime} ,r_{i}{\prime} } \right) ;\left( {i = 1,2, \ldots ,n} \right)$$\end{document} be a CIFSs such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{i} \le m_{i}{\prime} ; n_{i} \ge n_{i}{\prime} ;r_{i} \le r_{i}{\prime} , \forall i$$\end{document}. Then,

17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$CIFAABM_{\Delta }^{\ell ,m} \left( { C_{1} ,C_{2} , \ldots , C_{n } } \right) \le CIFAABM_{\Delta }^{\ell ,m} \left( {C _{1}{\prime} ,C _{2}{\prime} , \ldots ,C _{n}{\prime} } \right)$$\end{document}

Proof:

See Appendix C.

Theorem 4: (Commutativity)

Consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{i} = \left( {m_{i} ,n_{i} ,r_{i} } \right)$$\end{document}

and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{i}{\prime} = \left( {m_{i}{\prime} ,n_{i}{\prime} ,r_{i}{\prime} } \right) ;\left( {i = 1,2, \ldots ,n} \right)$$\end{document} be a CIFS such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{i} \le m_{i}{\prime} , n_{i} \ge n_{i}{\prime} ,r_{i} \le r_{i}{\prime} , \forall i$$\end{document}. Then,

18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$CIFAABM_{\Delta }^{\ell ,m} \left( { C_{1} ,C_{2} , \ldots , C_{n} } \right) = CIFAABM_{\Delta }^{\ell ,m} \left( {C _{1}{\prime} ,C _{2}{\prime} , \ldots ,C _{n}{\prime} } \right)$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {C{ }_{1}{\prime} ,C{ }_{2}{\prime} , \ldots ,C{ }_{n}{\prime} } \right)$$\end{document}. is any permutation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {C _{1} ,C _{2} , \ldots ,C _{n} } \right).$$\end{document}

Theorem 5: (Boundedness)

Consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{\mathsf{i}}=\left({\text{m}}_{\mathsf{i}},{\text{n}}_{\mathsf{i}},{r}_{\mathsf{i}}\right)$$\end{document} be a collection of CIFS and.

19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{C }_{\mathsf{i}}}^{-}\le {{\text{CIFAABM}}_{\Delta }}^{{\ell},\mathcal{m}}\left( {C}_{1},{C}_{2},\dots , {C}_{\mathsf{n} }\right)\le {{C }_{\mathsf{i}}}^{-}$$\end{document}

Proof:

See Appendix D.

Now, consider the following cases by putting the different values of parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{m}$$\end{document}.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{m}\to 0$$\end{document}, then (Eq. 19)20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\underset{\mathcal{m}\to 0}{\text{lim}}CIFAABM}_{\Delta }}^{{\ell},\mathcal{m}}\left( {C}_{1},{C}_{2},\dots , {C}_{\mathsf{n} }\right)=\underset{\mathcal{m}\to 0}{\text{lim}}{\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{n} \left(\mathsf{n} -1\right)}\left({\oplus }_{\begin{array}{c}r,s=1\\ r\ne s\end{array}}^{\mathsf{n}}\left({C}_{\mathbb{r}}^{{\ell}}\otimes {C}_{\mathbb{s}}^{\mathcal{m}}\right)\right)\right)}^{\frac{1}{{\ell}+\mathcal{m}}}={\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{n} }\left({\oplus }_{\begin{array}{c}r,s=1\\ r\ne s\end{array}}^{\mathsf{n}}{C}_{\mathbb{r}}^{{\ell}}\right)\right)}^{\frac{1}{{\ell}}}$$\end{document}

Which is known as circular intuitionistic fuzzy Aczel Alsina Generalized Bonferroni Mean (CIFAAGBM) Operator.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell}=1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{m}\to 0$$\end{document}, then (Eq. 19)21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\underset{\mathcal{m}\to 0}{\text{lim}}CIFAABM}_{\Delta }}^{\text{1,1}}\left( {C}_{1},{C}_{2},\dots , {C}_{\mathsf{n} }\right)=\underset{{\ell},\mathcal{m}\to 1}{\text{lim}}{\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{n} \left(\mathsf{n} -1\right)}\left({\oplus }_{\begin{array}{c}r,s=1\\ r\ne s\end{array}}^{\mathsf{n}}\left({C}_{\mathbb{r}}^{{\ell}}\otimes {C}_{\mathbb{s}}^{\mathcal{m}}\right)\right)\right)}^\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\underset{\mathcal{m}\to 0}{\text{lim}}CIFAABM}_{\Delta }}^{\text{1,0}}\left( {C}_{1},{C}_{2},\dots , {C}_{\mathsf{n} }\right)=\frac{1}{\mathsf{n} }\left({\oplus }_{\begin{array}{c}r,s=1\\ r\ne s\end{array}}^{\mathsf{n}}{C}_{\mathbb{r}}^{1}\right)$$\end{document}

Which is known as circular intuitionistic fuzzy Aczel Alsina Bonferroni Average Mean (CIFAABAM) Operator.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell}=2$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{m}\to 0,$$\end{document} then (Eq. 19)23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\underset{\mathcal{m}\to 0}{\text{lim}}CIFAABM}_{\Delta }}^{\text{2,0}}\left( {C}_{1},{C}_{2},\dots , {C}_{\mathsf{n} }\right)={\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{n} }\left({\oplus }_{\begin{array}{c}r,s=1\\ r\ne s\end{array}}^{\mathsf{n}}{C}_{\mathbb{r}}^{2}\right)\right)}^\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}

Which is known as circular intuitionistic fuzzy Aczel Alsina Bonferroni Square Mean (CIFAABSM) Operator.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell}=1=\mathcal{m},$$\end{document} then (Eq. 19)

Which is known circular intuitionistic fuzzy Aczel Alsina Bonferroni interrelated Square Mean (CIFAABISM) Operator.

Assessment of the PL model based on the proposed methodology

In this section, we’ll discuss the MCGDM problem based on the proposed operator (CIFAABM) utilizing the ∆ and ∇, and determine the most suitable technique for enhancing the PL and demonstrating its accuracy and effectiveness.

MCGDM Methodology based on the proposed operator

Consider a collection of finite set of alternatives and criteria \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{A}=\left\{{\mathcal{A}}_{1},{\mathcal{A}}_{2},\dots ,{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathsf{n}}\right\}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{P}=\left\{{\mathcal{P}}_{1},{\mathcal{P}}_{2},\dots ,{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{m}}\right\}$$\end{document} respectively, having weight vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{w}={\left({\mathcal{w}}_{1},{\mathcal{w}}_{2},\dots ,{\mathcal{w}}_{\mathsf{n}}\right)}^{\mathcal{t}}$$\end{document}, s.t \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{\mathcal{j}=1}^{\mathsf{n}}{\mathcal{w}}_{\mathcal{j}}=1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{w}}_{\mathcal{j}}>0 , \forall \mathcal{j}$$\end{document}. To evaluate the best technique for enhancing the PL in improving the comprehension skills of secondary school students, multiple techniques are evaluated in a proposed environment where each expertise is restricted for assigning values to each alternative in the form of CIFN \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{\mathsf{i}}=\left({m}_{\mathsf{i}},{n}_{\mathsf{i}},{r}_{\mathsf{i}}\right)$$\end{document}.

Normalize the matrix if it cost type otherwise don’t need to be normalized if all the criteria are identical, by this Eq.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak{r}}_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}{c}{\overline{C} }_{ij} ; cost type\\ {C}_{ij} ; benefit type\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{C} }_{ij}$$\end{document} is the complement of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{ij}$$\end{document} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left({{n}_{\mathsf{i}},m}_{\mathsf{i}},1-{r}_{\mathsf{i}}\right)$$\end{document}.

Utilize the proposed operator (Eq. 21) to accumulate the decision matrices.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{gathered} CIFAABM_{\Delta } ^{{\ell ,m}} \left( {~C_{1} ,C_{2} , \ldots ,~C_{{n~}} } \right) = \left( {e^{{ - \left( {\frac{1}{{\ell + m}}\left( { - \ln \left( {1 - e^{{ - \left( {\frac{1}{{n~\left( {n~ - 1} \right)}}\left( { - \ln \left( {1 - \left( {1 - \left( {e^{\xi } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} } \right)} \right)} \right)^{ \propto } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} }} } \right)} \right)^{ \propto } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} }} ,} \right. \hfill \\ \left. { - e^{{ - \left( {\frac{1}{{\ell + m}}\left( \rangle \right)^{ \propto } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} }} ,e^{{ - \left( {\frac{1}{{\ell + m}}\left( { - \ln \left( {1 - e^{{ - \left( {\frac{1}{{n~\left( {n~ - 1} \right)}}\left( { - \ln \left( {1 - \left( {\left( {1 - \left( {e^{\psi } } \right)} \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} } \right)} \right)} \right)^{ \propto } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} }} } \right)} \right)^{ \propto } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} }} } \right) \hfill \\ \end{gathered}$$\end{document}

Utilize the proposed operator (Eq. 21) to accumulate the decision matrices.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{gathered} CIFAABM_{\Delta } ^{{\ell ,m}} \left( {~C_{1} ,C_{2} , \ldots ,~C_{{n~}} } \right) = \left( {e^{{ - \left( {\frac{1}{{\ell + m}}\left( { - \ln \left( {1 - e^{{ - \left( {\frac{1}{{n~\left( {n~ - 1} \right)}}\left( { - \ln \left( {1 - \left( {1 - \left( {e^{\xi } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} } \right)} \right)} \right)^{ \propto } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} }} } \right)} \right)^{ \propto } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} }} ,} \right. \hfill \\ \left. { - e^{{ - \left( {\frac{1}{{\ell + m}}\left( \rangle \right)^{ \propto } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} }} ,e^{{ - \left( {\frac{1}{{\ell + m}}\left( { - \ln \left( {1 - e^{{ - \left( {\frac{1}{{n~\left( {n~ - 1} \right)}}\left( { - \ln \left( {1 - \left( {\left( {1 - \left( {e^{\psi } } \right)} \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} } \right)} \right)} \right)^{ \propto } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} }} } \right)} \right)^{ \propto } } \right)^{{\frac{1}{ \propto }}} }} } \right) \hfill \\ \end{gathered}$$\end{document}

Compute the values of each alternative by utilizing the accumulated decision matrices.

Calculate the score value (Eq. 2) of each alternative and rank them to select the best optimal choice.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{S}}_{C}=\left({m}_{\mathsf{i}}-{n}_{\mathsf{i}}\right)\times {r}_{\mathsf{i}}$$\end{document}

Flowchart of the methodology.

Assessment of PL system

The primary goal of this study is to determine the key component of PL models by using the various investigated criteria. We aim to assess PL models based on several criteria, including their effectiveness, student engagement, adaptability, and overall impact on learning outcomes. For this analysis, we selected the five best key components (alternatives), which are shown below:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{A}}_{1}=Fully PL system$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{A}}_{2}=Partially Semi PL system$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{A}}_{3}=Hybrid PL system$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{A}}_{4}=Teacher led PL system$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{A}}_{5}=Technology integration PL system$$\end{document}

For this case study, the data are hypothetically generated to provide a real-life educational situation and are used only to illustrate the real-life implementation of the proposed model.

A fully PL system in which all aspects of learning are entirely personalized to each student’s needs, with advanced AI and data analytics enabling continual adaptation. In a partial PL system, it blends traditional teaching methods with PL components while providing some customization based on student performance and preferences. In a hybrid PL system, both face-to-face and digital learning allow some degree of PL and flexibility in the learning environment. In a teacher-led PL system, teachers play an important role in tailoring learning experiences based on observations and student interactions. In \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{A}}_{5},$$\end{document} a traditional learning framework is added with technology capabilities to provide some amount of customization without completely transforming the system.

All these criteria play an essential role in the PL model and enhance student engagement, adaptability, and overall impact on learning outcomes but we have to point out the best alternative from the defined ones by considering the weight vector i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{w}={\left(\text{0.31,0.23,0.18,0.28}\right)}^{\mathcal{t}}$$\end{document}. To achieve our objective, we will consider the following criteria including:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{P}}_{1}=indiviualized learning path,$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{P}}_{2}=Competency based progression,$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{P}}_{3}=Teacher facilitaton,$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{P}}_{4}=Collaborative learning.$$\end{document}

The criteria chosen for evaluation, i.e. individualized learning path, competency-based progression, teacher facilitation, and collaborative learning, are interdependent within PL settings. For example, while learning paths work effectively when supported continuously by teachers, competency-based progression is supported by group learning with peer feedback. These relationships suggest that the influence of one criterion is not explainable in isolation. Therefore, the use of this Bonferroni Mean in our CIFAABM operator is justified since it reflects synergy and redundancy between criteria. This enables the accumulation of scores in a more nuanced manner and provides a more accurate representation of the overall effectiveness of each learning model.

cost-type criteria, it doesn’t require normalization.By using Eq. 21, the accumulated decision matrix is shown in Table 6.Aggregation of the accumulated decision matrix is shown in Table 7.Calculation Represents the PL model, which we have to evaluate, and by using the proposed operator, we get that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${a}_{5}$$\end{document} is the best PL model (alternative).

Evaluation of PL by assigning different values to parameters

The primary goal of the proposed AO is to demonstrate the interaction between each criterion value and to manage any uncertain value provided by experts, which may have an impact on the conclusion. By altering the values provided to each criterion, we aim to illustrate the efficiency and flexibility of the proposed operators in the context of evaluating PL models. By varying the specified values, we can see how each alternative performs under different circumstances and conditions. This method not only emphasizes the robustness of the evaluation process but also assures its effectiveness and validity. Evaluation of PL by assigning the different values to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{l}},{\varvec{m}}$$\end{document}

Evaluation of PL by assigning different values.

Comparison analysis

In Table 10, we compare the prior approach to the proposed method, which reveals that earlier approaches are frequently insufficient and fail to show the relationship between attributive values. To close this gap, we introduced the CIFAABM AO, which helps us make better decisions. The proposed approaches allow a more accurate and comprehensive evaluation by successfully clarifying the relationship between the criteria, which leads to a more dependable and correct selection of tailored learning models, thus boosting the quality and efficacy of PL. Moreover, the same can be said about the change in the extent of the impact depicted by the alternative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{A}}_{5}$$\end{document} as equal to − 0.135 to 0.018, which has recommended that the right parameters should always be selected to identify accurate and reliable decisions. The analysis highlights the introduced operator’s relative advantage in more accurately taking into account different levels of importance and interdependence of criteria compared to other approaches.

In MCDM approaches, the superiority of a model is not a function of the magnitude of the final score, but of its capacity to reveal nuanced interdependencies among criteria and consistently rank alternatives. A higher numerical score does not mean a better or more precise evaluation especially when it comes to complex decision environments, like PL where various factors contribute towards a result concurrently. The significant strength of the proposed CIFAABM operator is the fact that it can capture these interrelated influences by a combination of the BM and the A-A function. This combination enables the model to examine the interrelationships of criteria such as engagement, flexibility, and instructional quality, which results in more realistic as well as informative ranks.

Result discussion

The assessment of the proposed CIFAABM AO is stated in Table 10 in terms of comparison analysis with the other AOs, including the IFS, circular intuitionistic fuzzy Bonferroni mean (CIFBM), circular intuitionistic fuzzy Aczel–Alsina (CIFAA), and circular intuitionistic fuzzy Aczel–Alsina Bonferroni mean (CIFAABM). These findings indicate that the CIFAABM AO offers superior and more stable performance in all the tested settings concerning the analysis of interdependencies of criteria, which may have a non-linear character. For example, comparing the results of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{A}}_{1}$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{A}}_{5}$$\end{document} where they signify different performance levels of the studied operators, a sensitive comparison of these interactions is provided from CIFAABM’s perspective, as seen for Δ. Moreover, the traditional operators may not be capturing the dynamics of practice inherent in context, especially in the PL environments. So, this AO emphasizes the importance of developing other forms of AOs, such as CIFAABM, which can address the vagueness and imprecision that exist in educational information. Such a general comparison emphasizes the advantage and capability of CIFAABM AO formulation in offering a more appropriate and rational evaluation framework for multiple criteria decision-making issues in PL contexts.

To further corroborate the findings of the proposed approach, we employed real-world datasets with student engagement and performance data, particularly in secondary and higher education. To ensure that the CIFAABM AO is assessed effectively, these datasets were selected to cover various educational settings. Thus, to compare the impact of different AOs, we performed the comparison analysis and considered the score values for accuracy evaluation. That confirmed the fact that the CIFAABM AO performs significantly higher than traditional AOs, including CIFS, CIFWA, CIFWG, CIFBM, and CIFAA, particularly in cases where interactions between criteria are crucial. Moreover, to calculate the result outcomes of the CIFWA and CIFWG, the equal weights value (hypothetical) is considered. This robust validation framework shows the advantage of the CIFAABM AO over other approaches in the presence of ambiguity and uncertainty in education data. Being a highly versatile and accurate assessment tool, the CIFAABM AO is a crucial tool for educators and decision-makers, providing a more accurate and detailed look at how MCGDM problems can be approached and solved, especially in the sphere of personalization.

Practical implementation of PL assessment for educators using CIFAABM AO

The following are the practical implications for educators using CIFAABM AO:CIFAABM AO can be used to determine different learning preferences and levels of participation so that the teacher can develop lessons to meet their students’ needs.It leverages the operator’s capacity to handle multiple interconnections and interdependencies of a skill to develop contextualized learning models that adapt according to the student’s capabilities and challenges.Develop differential interaction modalities (i.e., whole class activities as opposed to self-paced work) based on CIFAABM AO outputs helping create more appropriate classroom interactions.Use the results of CIFAABM AO to make proper choices regarding the material to be introduced in the curriculum, as well as the pedagogical approaches for students with diverse learning abilities.Use comprehensive feedback and support, improve the interaction between the teacher and the student, and focus on creating a favorable learning climate.The second approach is to train educators to use other sophisticated AOs so that the teachers can create customized evaluation criteria that reflect the individual progress of each student, rather than relying solely on standardized tests.

Conclusion

This paper was intended to improve the assessment of PL models by proposing the CIFAABM AO. Compared with the existing methods, which have been discussed above and are typically incapable of handling interactions and coupling in PL assessment contexts, the aforementioned CIFAABM operator can be seen as a reasonable solution for qualitatively evaluating such models based on several criteria. These results present evidence that the use of the CIFAABM operator enhances the accuracy of decision-making by managing the vagueness and uncertainty inherent in FS data. The simulations performed indicate that this operator can yield better results compared to other aggregation techniques, especially when the interdependence of criteria is essential. For example, based on this quantitative study, we found out that our proposed CIFAABM operator is not only effective in identifying the most suitable PL model but also stable and reliable in different test scenarios. These findings support the use of upper-level AOs such as CIFAABM for MCGDM issues, particularly in education. Due to the capacity of CIFAABM to handle complex and ambiguous data, this approach is significant for educators and decision-makers who seek to enhance PL environments. So, for the assessment of the PL model, the CIFAABM AO offers a more nuanced evaluation by effectively handling multiple criteria and their interdependencies. By using the proposed operator, the specific outcomes are obtained as follows;The proposed AO assists the decision-maker in improving the measure of the likelihood of the PL model to improve the various learnability criteria such as learner participation rate, learner performance prediction, and the learning modes. This accuracy is advantageous to educators as it leads to the development of better strategies and enhanced student performance immediately.The work demonstrated that for a variety of test conditions, the operator of CIFAABM remains smooth and accurate including when data is noisy or ambiguous. This robustness is convenient when learning in many conditions present in classroom-based training as data may be noisy or of varying quality. This is quite essential, as it attempts to exclude the possibility of misjudgment among educators and thus, mostly rely on this method for making the right decisions.The evaluations became accurate because of the operator’s capacity to deal with the vagueness and uncertainty inherent in CIFS value. This capability is considered essential because mostly there is not enough clear data in an educational situation and an expert has to choose among potential scenarios. To support the more distinct meaning or clarification, the decision analysis operator assists in participating in modifying the teaching approaches in the learning instructions that are useful in addressing various students.

This accuracy is advantageous to educators as it leads to the development of better strategies and enhanced student performance immediately.

Advantages

The MCGDM approach evaluates PL learning models by considering multiple criteria to ensure a comprehensive assessment.By assigning different values to the criteria, the technique can be tailored to diverse educational settings and priorities, offering versatility and flexibility.The utilization of data analytics improves the accuracy and effectiveness of the evaluation, resulting in more accurate conclusions.The findings support the use of highly PL systems, which have the potential to boost student engagement and learning outcomes significantly.

The MCGDM approach evaluates PL learning models by considering multiple criteria to ensure a comprehensive assessment.

Limitation and sensitive analysis

The proposed model has the potential to be applied across different educational contexts due to its flexibility in handling uncertainty and its ability to aggregate various inputs, such as varying student learning styles and performance metrics. This makes it a versatile tool for personalized learning in various settings, from traditional classrooms to online education. However, there are limitations to consider.The model may require significant customization to address the unique needs of different educational environments. For instance, the specific criteria and membership values used in the model may need to be adjusted based on the student population, subject matter, or cultural context. Additionally, implementing this model at scale might pose challenges, such as the need for substantial data collection and analysis capabilities, as well as training educators to effectively use the model.It is the success of the evaluation, based on the records existent for each criterion. The accuracy of the results may be radically affected if the data part of it is incomplete or contains errors. In situations where the data is highly uncertain and conflicting the model’s volatility might produce less reliable results.The relative importance of the features is subjective and such importance can be divergent from one person to another. This would somehow create inconsistency in the results especially where the management is operating in a highly uncertain or conflicting context. This problem may be solved by the readjustment of the weights or by adopting other forms of adaptive weight tuning.Fully personalized learning systems however take a lot of planning and implementation with proper technology and s more often than not a fiscal feat that may not be achievable, especially with schools and colleges. However, these might prove complex to implement especially in its computational aspect as may be seen in the CIFAABM AO model.It may challenge to scale up personalized learning, particularly in issues to do with quality and standard. However, for extreme situations with mixed or unclear information, it is recommended to use the CIFAABM AO along with other types of analysis of the management decision.When there is a lot of conflicting data or data is highly uncertain and the values are not in the circular environment then the effectiveness of the CIFAABM AO may decline. Further studies could therefore extend CIFAABM by adopting additional sound approaches to decision-making which may perhaps involve consensus models or machine learning for handling such complications.

The model may require significant customization to address the unique needs of different educational environments. For instance, the specific criteria and membership values used in the model may need to be adjusted based on the student population, subject matter, or cultural context. Additionally, implementing this model at scale might pose challenges, such as the need for substantial data collection and analysis capabilities, as well as training educators to effectively use the model.

It is the success of the evaluation, based on the records existent for each criterion. The accuracy of the results may be radically affected if the data part of it is incomplete or contains errors. In situations where the data is highly uncertain and conflicting the model’s volatility might produce less reliable results.

The relative importance of the features is subjective and such importance can be divergent from one person to another. This would somehow create inconsistency in the results especially where the management is operating in a highly uncertain or conflicting context. This problem may be solved by the readjustment of the weights or by adopting other forms of adaptive weight tuning.

When there is a lot of conflicting data or data is highly uncertain and the values are not in the circular environment then the effectiveness of the CIFAABM AO may decline. Further studies could therefore extend CIFAABM by adopting additional sound approaches to decision-making which may perhaps involve consensus models or machine learning for handling such complications.

When there is a lot of conflicting data or data is highly uncertain and the values are not in the circular environment then the effectiveness of the CIFAABM AO may decline. Further studies could therefore extend CIFAABM by adopting additional sound approaches to decision-making which may perhaps involve consensus models or machine learning for handling such complications.

To overcome these limitations, some modifications can be made by adding the localized criteria, and providing professional development for educators to ensure they can apply the model effectively. Another adaptation could involve integrating the model with existing educational technologies to facilitate its use in various settings, making it more accessible and easier to implement on a larger scale.

Future direction

In the future, these studies could be extended by incorporating the CIFAABM AO with new technologies such as artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML) to boost the reliability of PL models. For example, integrating CIFAABM AO with other intelligent algorithms such as neural networks or clustering, might be useful in creating dynamic models that adjust to the shifting needs and behaviour of learners over time. Moreover, the proposed approach could be used in other educational settings like higher education, vocational training, e-learning, etc., where a decision-making process requires working with multiple and variable criteria. Also, further studies can compare diverse AOs like Dombi’s AO, Hamming Mean, Einstein AO, Muirhead mean43, Extended BM44 operator to understand how these can effectively solve problems related to ambiguity in the context of PL, like extremely ambiguous data, etc. These advanced operators could offer richer and context-aware decision-making paradigms, which in turn would extend the scalability and utility of the PL models in diverse educational contexts.

Moreover, the proposed model can be defined as a Neutrosophic framework45, soft set framework46, hyper soft framework47, type-2 fuzzy framework48, linguistic 2-tuples49 and can be assessed by the VIKOR method50, CRADIS Approach51, Edas Approach52, information measures53, TS fuzzy systems54, fuzzy neural network55, and by metric spaces56.
